What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Identify an unclear measurement reference, correct an assumption, and obtain designer clarification before treating the dimension as usable passage.
  • Compare specified material, appearance, profile, and quoted lead time without inventing quality rankings or selecting for the client.
  • Identify a drawing conflict precisely, preserve revision and location details, and request clarification without choosing a dimension by convenience.
  • Describe an exposed condition, explain what cannot be seen, and refer a structural concern without diagnosing concealed timber.
  • Explain the agreed task, clarify an additional request, and discuss a separate quotation without implying approval or completion timing.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All clients, drawings, materials, dimensions, products, prices, schedules, and site conditions in these cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not structural assessment, building-code compliance, tool operation, demolition, installation, or legal interpretation. Follow actual project documents, manufacturer instructions, site controls, competent assessments, and applicable requirements. The scenarios do not authorize fabrication from conflicting drawings, entry to unreleased areas, concealed-condition work, substitutions, extra work, or changes to warranty terms.

01Confirming openings and measurement referencesWhat does 900 millimeters describe?+

Your goal

Identify an unclear measurement reference, correct an assumption, and obtain designer clarification before treating the dimension as usable passage.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

What does 900 millimeters describe?

Client Hana and carpenter Ellis review a hall drawing labeled door opening: 900 millimeters. Hana assumes the figure describes clear passage width. The note does not identify a rough or finished opening, and no door has been ordered. Ellis must clarify the ambiguity without inventing a frame allowance or asserting a usable width. Designer Priya can explain which reference the dimension uses. The conversation concerns interpretation and referral, not a construction method or a compliance decision.

Hana: Client / Ellis: Carpenter

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the drawing label say?

Answer and explanation

B. Door opening: 900 millimeters, without a rough or finished reference

The note supplies a number but does not identify the opening reference.

2. What is Hana assuming?

Answer and explanation

D. That 900 millimeters is clear passage width

Hana reads the number as usable passage, which has not been established.

3. What is the appropriate next step?

Answer and explanation

B. Ask Priya to clarify the measurement reference

Priya can clarify the reference; the conversation does not establish an order size or compliance.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The only established figure is 900 millimeters in an unclear opening note. Rough opening, finished opening, door size, and clear passage are not interchangeable labels. No conversion or allowance is supplied, and no door has been ordered.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
opening dimension
Measurement describing an opening using a stated reference.clarify the opening dimension
rough opening
Structural opening before the door assembly and relevant finishes are installed.confirm the rough-opening reference
finished opening
Opening measured at the specified completed surfaces.identify the finished opening
clear passage
Unobstructed usable space through an opening under the stated conditions.confirm the clear passage
door leaf
Moving panel of a door.distinguish the door leaf
door frame
Surrounding assembly supporting and locating the door.identify the door frame
jamb
Frame member; side jambs and the head jamb identify different positions.identify the side jamb
head
Upper horizontal member or part of an opening.identify the frame head
threshold
Lower part at the base of a doorway.refer to the threshold
casing
Trim around a door or window opening.identify the casing
architrave
Term commonly used for decorative trim around an opening.compare the architrave profile
millimeter
Metric unit equal to one thousandth of a meter.state the dimension in millimeters
dimension line
Drawing line indicating the extent of a measurement.follow the dimension line
reference face
Surface from which a dimension is measured.identify the reference face
datum
Stated reference point, line, or level for measurements.confirm the datum
nominal size
Designated size that may differ from an actual measured size.distinguish nominal size
actual dimension
Measured or specifically defined physical size.verify the actual dimension
frame allowance
Space allowed for the specified frame arrangement.verify the frame allowance
clearance
Space between components or around an operating part.clarify the required clearance
door schedule
Drawing or document listing specified doors and their attributes.check the door schedule
drawing note
Written instruction or information on a drawing.clarify the drawing note
designer clarification
Explanation from the responsible design professional.request designer clarification
order size
Dimensions used to specify a product for purchase.confirm the order size
dimension reference
Point, surface, or condition a measurement describes.establish the dimension reference

Ask what the measurement runs between and what stage of construction it describes. A clarification can resolve the drawing meaning; it does not by itself establish a code-compliance finding. Do not calculate a usable width from an invented frame thickness.

C Notice the language

A precise number can still carry an unclear meaning

A client may reasonably read an opening dimension as the space available to pass through. Acknowledge that interpretation, explain the missing reference in ordinary words, and ask the designer to clarify it. Do not make uncertainty sound like a mistake the client should have recognized.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the note
The hall drawing labels the door opening as 900 millimeters.
Clarify the assumption
Are you reading that as the clear passage width?
Name the ambiguity
The note does not say rough or finished opening.
Explain the distinction
The opening reference and the usable passage are not necessarily the same.
Ask about the reference
What surfaces does this dimension run between?
Keep the number intact
I am not changing the figure of 900 millimeters.
Avoid inventing an allowance
We do not have a confirmed frame allowance here.
Preserve the order status
No door has been ordered.
Refer the interpretation
Priya can clarify the measurement reference.
Avoid a usable-width promise
I cannot confirm 900 millimeters of clear passage from this note.
Distinguish product size
We should not treat this as the order size yet.
Ask for explicit wording
Could the clarification identify the opening type?
Preserve the client need
I will explain that your question concerns usable passage.
Avoid a compliance claim
This conversation does not establish compliance.
Read back the query
Does 900 refer to the rough opening, finished opening, or another reference?
Close with the next step
I will request clarification before presenting the dimension as a confirmed usable width.
6 language and precision notes
Labels versus establishes
A drawing label states a number but may not establish its precise reference.
Clear
Describes unobstructed space, not merely a named opening size.
Run between
Natural phrasing for the endpoints of a dimension.
Not necessarily
Rejects an unsupported equivalence without asserting a specific alternative.
No order yet
Prevents the clarification discussion from sounding like a change to a placed order.
Reference
Names what the number actually measures, rather than questioning arithmetic.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question targets the missing information?

Answer and explanation

A. Does 900 millimeters refer to the rough opening, finished opening, or another reference?

The question asks what the stated dimension describes without assuming an interpretation.

2. Which reassurance is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

C. You will definitely have 900 millimeters of usable passage.

The note does not establish usable passage, so the reassurance exceeds the evidence.

3. What should remain unchanged in the query?

Answer and explanation

C. The stated 900-millimeter figure and the uncertainty about its reference

The query preserves the actual figure while asking for its unresolved meaning.

4. Which distinction matters for ordering?

Answer and explanation

C. An unclear opening note is not a confirmed door order size.

The dimension reference must be clarified before it can support an appropriate product specification.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

What does 900 millimeters describe?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Client / Ellis: Carpenter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • opening dimension
  • actual dimension
  • order size
  • drawing note
  • designer clarification
  • clear passage
  • dimension reference
  • rough opening
  • reference face
  • door frame
  1. Hana
    Before we order the hall door, can I check this 900-millimeter note? I'm expecting that much space to walk through with the door open.
  2. Ellis
    The 1 is missing. It says door opening, but doesn't identify a rough opening, finished opening, or another measurement. I can't promise the walking space from that.
  3. Hana
    Right, I'm interested in the gap I can actually use, not the width of the door leaf. Have I been reading the wrong measurement?
  4. Ellis
    You're asking about 2. It's a different question from the door's size, and the note doesn't establish that usable width.
  5. Hana
    When you say rough, do you mean the measurement isn't accurate? I'd prefer an exact figure before we choose anything.
  6. Ellis
    No, 3 describes the structural opening before the assembly and relevant finishes. Rough names a construction stage here, not a rough guess.
  7. Hana
    I see. So which surfaces does this particular arrow measure between? The drawing isn't clear enough for me to tell.
  8. Ellis
    That's the missing 4 information. We need the surfaces identified; subtracting a guessed allowance could give you a very misleading answer.
  9. Hana
    Has anyone already bought a door against that number? I'd rather raise it now than discover it doesn't give us the passage we need.
  10. Ellis
    No door is ordered, and the 5 isn't confirmed. We're clarifying the drawing before purchasing, not changing an existing order.
  11. Hana
    Can Priya settle the meaning? She prepared the design, and I'd like her to understand why I'm asking about the space.
  12. Ellis
    I'll request 6 from Priya and include your question about walking through the doorway. She needs both the note and the practical concern.
  13. Hana
    Please don't change 900 to a different number in the message. I'm asking what it means, not instructing her to redesign it.
  14. Ellis
    Agreed. I'll quote the 7 of 900 millimeters exactly and ask which construction stage and measurement surfaces it refers to.
  15. Hana
    Would the frame take some of that space? I'm not asking you to work it out now, but I don't want it overlooked.
  16. Ellis
    The 8 is a separate component to consider. Its dimensions aren't supplied here, so I won't invent a thickness or a resulting clear width.
  17. Hana
    And we're not confirming that the doorway meets a particular access requirement today? I don't want to pass that on as settled.
  18. Ellis
    Correct. Resolving the 9 isn't a compliance assessment. We'll keep that distinction clear when we pass the question to Priya.
  19. Hana
    Then the message is: what does 900 measure, and what usable passage will this arrangement give? No order until the reference is resolved.
  20. Ellis
    Yes. The 10 of that passage remains unverified. I'll send the query with the original note, without promising a width or a product size.

Answers and explanations
  1. dimension reference Turn 2. Dimension reference identifies what the 900-millimeter figure measures, which the current note does not make clear.
  2. clear passage Turn 4. Clear passage is the usable unobstructed space Hana wants to understand, not automatically the dimension on the drawing.
  3. rough opening Turn 6. Rough opening refers to the structural opening before the assembly and relevant finishes, not an approximate estimate of usable width.
  4. reference face Turn 8. Reference face names a surface used for measurement; the drawing note does not identify those surfaces clearly.
  5. order size Turn 10. Order size is not established by the ambiguous note, and no door has been ordered in this case.
  6. designer clarification Turn 12. Designer clarification is the appropriate route for resolving the intended measurement reference without a guessed interpretation.
  7. opening dimension Turn 14. Opening dimension remains the stated 900 millimeters; the query concerns its meaning rather than a requested numerical change.
  8. door frame Turn 16. Door frame is a distinct component whose dimensions must not be silently equated with usable passage or the ambiguous opening note.
  9. drawing note Turn 18. Drawing note needs an explanation of its measurement reference; the clarification conversation is not a compliance assessment.
  10. actual dimension Turn 20. Actual dimension of the usable passage is not verified; the next step is clarification, not a width guarantee or product order.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: Before we order the hall door, can I check this 900-millimeter note? I'm expecting that much space to walk through with the door open.
  2. Ellis: The dimension reference is missing. It says door opening, but doesn't identify a rough opening, finished opening, or another measurement. I can't promise the walking space from that.
  3. Hana: Right, I'm interested in the gap I can actually use, not the width of the door leaf. Have I been reading the wrong measurement?
  4. Ellis: You're asking about clear passage. It's a different question from the door's size, and the note doesn't establish that usable width.
  5. Hana: When you say rough, do you mean the measurement isn't accurate? I'd prefer an exact figure before we choose anything.
  6. Ellis: No, rough opening describes the structural opening before the assembly and relevant finishes. Rough names a construction stage here, not a rough guess.
  7. Hana: I see. So which surfaces does this particular arrow measure between? The drawing isn't clear enough for me to tell.
  8. Ellis: That's the missing reference face information. We need the surfaces identified; subtracting a guessed allowance could give you a very misleading answer.
  9. Hana: Has anyone already bought a door against that number? I'd rather raise it now than discover it doesn't give us the passage we need.
  10. Ellis: No door is ordered, and the order size isn't confirmed. We're clarifying the drawing before purchasing, not changing an existing order.
  11. Hana: Can Priya settle the meaning? She prepared the design, and I'd like her to understand why I'm asking about the space.
  12. Ellis: I'll request designer clarification from Priya and include your question about walking through the doorway. She needs both the note and the practical concern.
  13. Hana: Please don't change 900 to a different number in the message. I'm asking what it means, not instructing her to redesign it.
  14. Ellis: Agreed. I'll quote the opening dimension of 900 millimeters exactly and ask which construction stage and measurement surfaces it refers to.
  15. Hana: Would the frame take some of that space? I'm not asking you to work it out now, but I don't want it overlooked.
  16. Ellis: The door frame is a separate component to consider. Its dimensions aren't supplied here, so I won't invent a thickness or a resulting clear width.
  17. Hana: And we're not confirming that the doorway meets a particular access requirement today? I don't want to pass that on as settled.
  18. Ellis: Correct. Resolving the drawing note isn't a compliance assessment. We'll keep that distinction clear when we pass the question to Priya.
  19. Hana: Then the message is: what does 900 measure, and what usable passage will this arrangement give? No order until the reference is resolved.
  20. Ellis: Yes. The actual dimension of that passage remains unverified. I'll send the query with the original note, without promising a width or a product size.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Nine hundred between which faces?

At the hall doorway, the carpenter and client clarify the fictional 900-millimeter note.

  1. Carpenter: The hall drawing says nine hundred millimeters. Were you expecting that much clear passage?
  2. Client: Yes, our cabinet needs that space, and I thought the number guaranteed it.
  3. Carpenter: The note doesn't say whether it measures the rough opening or finished opening.
  4. Client: I hadn't realized those were different. Does the jamb take up some space?
  5. Carpenter: The frame and open door affect usable passage; we need the selected unit's dimensions.
  6. Client: We haven't chosen the door yet. Can you still clarify the drawing today?
  7. Carpenter: I can ask the designer which surfaces the number refers to before ordering.
  8. Client: Please mention the cabinet, because usable width is the requirement that matters most.
  9. Carpenter: I'll separate your passage requirement from the unresolved drawing dimension in that question.
  10. Client: That helps. Let's leave the order pending until those two things agree.
The floor that is not finished yet

A carpenter checks a cabinet-height reference with the site coordinator.

  1. Carpenter: Your cabinet sketch gives a height, but which floor surface is the datum?
  2. Site coordinator: The finished floor should be the reference, not the exposed subfloor visible today.
  3. Carpenter: Thanks. The supplier's survey note says existing floor, which could mean something different.
  4. Site coordinator: That survey happened before the floor finish was selected. I see the problem.
  5. Carpenter: Could you confirm the current floor build-up with the designer before fabrication release?
  6. Site coordinator: Yes, and I'll ask them to identify the finished floor reference on the sketch.
  7. Carpenter: Please keep the earlier survey attached so the supplier can see what changed.
  8. Site coordinator: Should I describe the old height as wrong, or simply as needing reconciliation?
  9. Carpenter: Needing reconciliation is more accurate; the two documents may use different reference surfaces.
  10. Site coordinator: I'll request a coordinated dimension, with fabrication release still pending that response.
A width without its units

A carpenter and supplier resolve an ambiguous shelf enquiry by telephone.

  1. Carpenter: Your quotation lists the alcove shelf as thirty, but it doesn't show units.
  2. Supplier: That's thirty inches of nominal width. Were you reading the number as centimeters?
  3. Carpenter: I was checking because our site measurements are recorded in millimeters throughout this job.
  4. Supplier: Understood. Is your figure the wall opening or the finished shelf length requested?
  5. Carpenter: It's the opening measurement; the shelf size still needs confirmation against the detail.
  6. Supplier: Then I shouldn't simply convert thirty inches and treat that as your order.
  7. Carpenter: Exactly. Please label the quotation dimension and wait for the confirmed fabrication size.
  8. Supplier: I can revise the description now without releasing any material to the workshop.
  9. Carpenter: Please also retain the unit labels when you send the revised quotation over.
  10. Supplier: Done. It will say nominal width in inches, with fabrication dimensions awaiting confirmation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does 900 millimeters describe?
  1. Read turns 1-10, stressing opening, passage, and ordered when each distinction changes the meaning.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20; quote 900 exactly and keep Priya's clarification separate from a width guarantee.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify before naming the usable width

Complete the exchange about the hall drawing. Keep the given number, missing reference, unverified passage, and designer referral distinct.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "The drawing says ___ millimeters."

Answer and explanation

A. 900

The stated figure is 900 millimeters, although its reference is unclear.

2. Carpenter: "The note does not identify rough or ___ opening."

Answer and explanation

D. finished

Finished is the alternative opening reference the note does not clarify.

3. Client: "My concern is the clear ___."

Answer and explanation

B. passage

Passage identifies the usable space Hana wants to understand.

4. Carpenter: "I will ask the ___ to clarify the reference."

Answer and explanation

C. designer

The designer can clarify the intended reference without the carpenter inventing it.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming openings and measurement references
Original fictional case: Client Hana and carpenter Ellis review a hall drawing labeled door opening: 900 millimeters. Hana assumes the figure describes clear passage width. The note does not identify a rough or finished opening, and no door has been ordered. Ellis must clarify the ambiguity without inventing a frame allowance or asserting a usable width. Designer Priya can explain which reference the dimension uses. The conversation concerns interpretation and referral, not a construction method or a compliance decision.
Language workshop: What does 900 millimeters describe?
Communication goal: Identify an unclear measurement reference, correct an assumption, and obtain designer clarification before treating the dimension as usable passage.
Language guidance: The only established figure is 900 millimeters in an unclear opening note. Rough opening, finished opening, door size, and clear passage are not interchangeable labels. No conversion or allowance is supplied, and no door has been ordered.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The hall drawing labels the door opening as 900 millimeters. / Are you reading that as the clear passage width? / The note does not say rough or finished opening. / The opening reference and the usable passage are not necessarily the same. / What surfaces does this dimension run between? / I am not changing the figure of 900 millimeters. / We do not have a confirmed frame allowance here. / No door has been ordered. / Priya can clarify the measurement reference. / I cannot confirm 900 millimeters of clear passage from this note. / We should not treat this as the order size yet. / Could the clarification identify the opening type? / I will explain that your question concerns usable passage. / This conversation does not establish compliance. / Does 900 refer to the rough opening, finished opening, or another reference? / I will request clarification before presenting the dimension as a confirmed usable width.
Vocabulary: opening dimension: Measurement describing an opening using a stated reference. / rough opening: Structural opening before the door assembly and relevant finishes are installed. / finished opening: Opening measured at the specified completed surfaces. / clear passage: Unobstructed usable space through an opening under the stated conditions. / door leaf: Moving panel of a door. / door frame: Surrounding assembly supporting and locating the door. / jamb: Frame member; side jambs and the head jamb identify different positions. / head: Upper horizontal member or part of an opening. / threshold: Lower part at the base of a doorway. / casing: Trim around a door or window opening. / architrave: Term commonly used for decorative trim around an opening. / millimeter: Metric unit equal to one thousandth of a meter. / dimension line: Drawing line indicating the extent of a measurement. / reference face: Surface from which a dimension is measured. / datum: Stated reference point, line, or level for measurements. / nominal size: Designated size that may differ from an actual measured size. / actual dimension: Measured or specifically defined physical size. / frame allowance: Space allowed for the specified frame arrangement. / clearance: Space between components or around an operating part. / door schedule: Drawing or document listing specified doors and their attributes. / drawing note: Written instruction or information on a drawing. / designer clarification: Explanation from the responsible design professional. / order size: Dimensions used to specify a product for purchase. / dimension reference: Point, surface, or condition a measurement describes.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, stressing opening, passage, and ordered when each distinction changes the meaning. Switch roles for turns 11-20; quote 900 exactly and keep Priya's clarification separate from a width guarantee. Complete and check the four-line exchange. Read it aloud without subtracting an invented frame allowance.
Partner: Hana: Client / Ellis: Carpenter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the exchange about the hall drawing. Keep the given number, missing reference, unverified passage, and designer referral distinct.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: And we're not confirming that the doorway meets a particular access requirement today? I don't want to pass that on as settled.
Ellis: Correct. Resolving the drawing note isn't a compliance assessment. We'll keep that distinction clear when we pass the question to Priya.

END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Confirming openings and measurement references
Original fictional case: Client Hana and carpenter Ellis review a hall drawing labeled door opening: 900 millimeters. Hana assumes the figure describes clear passage width. The note does not identify a rough or finished opening, and no door has been ordered. Ellis must clarify the ambiguity without inventing a frame allowance or asserting a usable width. Designer Priya can explain which reference the dimension uses. The conversation concerns interpretation and referral, not a construction method or a compliance decision.
Language workshop: What does 900 millimeters describe?
Communication goal: Identify an unclear measurement reference, correct an assumption, and obtain designer clarification before treating the dimension as usable passage.
Language guidance: The only established figure is 900 millimeters in an unclear opening note. Rough opening, finished opening, door size, and clear passage are not interchangeable labels. No conversion or allowance is supplied, and no door has been ordered.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The hall drawing labels the door opening as 900 millimeters. / Are you reading that as the clear passage width? / The note does not say rough or finished opening. / The opening reference and the usable passage are not necessarily the same. / What surfaces does this dimension run between? / I am not changing the figure of 900 millimeters. / We do not have a confirmed frame allowance here. / No door has been ordered. / Priya can clarify the measurement reference. / I cannot confirm 900 millimeters of clear passage from this note. / We should not treat this as the order size yet. / Could the clarification identify the opening type? / I will explain that your question concerns usable passage. / This conversation does not establish compliance. / Does 900 refer to the rough opening, finished opening, or another reference? / I will request clarification before presenting the dimension as a confirmed usable width.
Vocabulary: opening dimension: Measurement describing an opening using a stated reference. / rough opening: Structural opening before the door assembly and relevant finishes are installed. / finished opening: Opening measured at the specified completed surfaces. / clear passage: Unobstructed usable space through an opening under the stated conditions. / door leaf: Moving panel of a door. / door frame: Surrounding assembly supporting and locating the door. / jamb: Frame member; side jambs and the head jamb identify different positions. / head: Upper horizontal member or part of an opening. / threshold: Lower part at the base of a doorway. / casing: Trim around a door or window opening. / architrave: Term commonly used for decorative trim around an opening. / millimeter: Metric unit equal to one thousandth of a meter. / dimension line: Drawing line indicating the extent of a measurement. / reference face: Surface from which a dimension is measured. / datum: Stated reference point, line, or level for measurements. / nominal size: Designated size that may differ from an actual measured size. / actual dimension: Measured or specifically defined physical size. / frame allowance: Space allowed for the specified frame arrangement. / clearance: Space between components or around an operating part. / door schedule: Drawing or document listing specified doors and their attributes. / drawing note: Written instruction or information on a drawing. / designer clarification: Explanation from the responsible design professional. / order size: Dimensions used to specify a product for purchase. / dimension reference: Point, surface, or condition a measurement describes.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, stressing opening, passage, and ordered when each distinction changes the meaning. Switch roles for turns 11-20; quote 900 exactly and keep Priya's clarification separate from a width guarantee. Complete and check the four-line exchange. Read it aloud without subtracting an invented frame allowance.
Partner: Hana: Client / Ellis: Carpenter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the exchange about the hall drawing. Keep the given number, missing reference, unverified passage, and designer referral distinct.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: And we're not confirming that the doorway meets a particular access requirement today? I don't want to pass that on as settled.
Ellis: Correct. Resolving the drawing note isn't a compliance assessment. We'll keep that distinction clear when we pass the question to Priya.
Hana: Then the message is: what does 900 measure, and what usable passage will this arrangement give? No order until the reference is resolved.
Ellis: Yes. The actual dimension of that passage remains unverified. I'll send the query with the original note, without promising a width or a product size.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Selecting timber, trim, and visible finishesTwo oak samples, different trade-offs+

Your goal

Compare specified material, appearance, profile, and quoted lead time without inventing quality rankings or selecting for the client.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Two oak samples, different trade-offs

For a study remodel, carpenter Mei compares two trim samples with client Oliver. Sample A is solid oak with pronounced grain and a quoted ten-day lead time. Sample B is oak-veneered trim with a more uniform appearance and a quoted four-day lead time. Both match the requested profile. No sample is selected, and no price comparison, durability ranking, or installation date is supplied. Mei must keep the comparison tied to these samples rather than generalize about every solid or veneered product.

Oliver: Client / Mei: Carpenter

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which description belongs to sample A?

Answer and explanation

C. Solid oak, pronounced grain, ten-day quoted lead time

Sample A has the specified solid-oak construction, pronounced grain, and ten-day quote.

2. What do the samples have in common?

Answer and explanation

C. Both match the requested profile

The shared attribute is the requested profile, not construction, appearance, or timing.

3. What remains undecided?

Answer and explanation

A. Which sample to select

No selection has been made, although the comparison attributes are stated.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Sample A has pronounced grain and a ten-day quoted lead time. Sample B has a more uniform appearance and a four-day quote. Both match the profile. These are sample-specific facts, not universal claims about solid oak and veneered products.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
solid oak
Material made from oak wood rather than an oak surface veneer over a core.specify solid oak trim
oak veneer
Thin oak layer applied over a supporting material.identify the oak veneer
veneered trim
Trim with a veneer surface over a core.compare veneered trim
timber
Wood prepared or used for building and joinery.specify the timber
trim
Finishing material used at edges, joints, or decorative features.compare the trim samples
profile
Cross-sectional shape of a trim piece or molding.match the requested profile
grain
Visible pattern associated with the wood's structure.compare the grain
pronounced grain
Strongly visible wood pattern.describe pronounced grain
uniform appearance
More consistent visual character across the sample.describe a uniform appearance
core material
Supporting material beneath a surface layer.identify the core material
face veneer
Veneer on the visible face of a product.specify the face veneer
edge treatment
Way the edge of a component is finished or covered.clarify the edge treatment
finish sample
Example showing a proposed surface appearance.review the finish sample
sheen
Degree of surface reflectivity.compare the sheen
clear finish
Coating that allows underlying material to remain visible.specify a clear finish
opaque finish
Coating that hides the underlying material's appearance.distinguish an opaque finish
stain
Coloring treatment used to change wood's appearance.clarify the stain color
quoted lead time
Supply interval stated in a quotation or proposal.compare quoted lead times
selection status
Whether a choice has been made.confirm the selection status
material construction
How a product is made up of its constituent materials.compare material construction
visual preference
Appearance favored by the client.clarify the visual preference
sample approval
Acceptance of a particular reference sample.record sample approval
performance claim
Statement about how a product will function or endure.avoid an unsupported performance claim
installation date
Scheduled day for fitting work at the site.confirm the installation date

No prices, durability results, finish specifications, or installation dates are given. A shorter lead time does not establish a lower price or better performance. Comparing options does not mean the client has approved either sample or placed an order.

C Notice the language

Compare attributes without selling an unsupported conclusion

A client may ask which product is better when the real choice involves several preferences. Compare the known attributes side by side and clarify the selection status. Avoid implying that a shorter quoted lead time means better quality or that a material name establishes every performance characteristic.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the comparison
Both samples match the requested profile.
Describe A's construction
Sample A is solid oak.
Describe B's construction
Sample B is oak-veneered trim.
Describe A's appearance
A has more pronounced grain.
Describe B's appearance
B has a more uniform appearance.
State A's timing
A has a quoted ten-day lead time.
State B's timing
B has a quoted four-day lead time.
Make a bounded comparison
B has the shorter quoted lead time for these two samples.
Avoid a price inference
We do not have a price comparison here.
Avoid a quality ranking
The material names alone do not establish which will perform better.
Preserve the shared feature
The profile is not the difference between them.
Clarify the preference
Is the more visible grain the appearance you prefer?
Separate discussion and selection
Discussing A does not mean you have selected it.
Keep the date distinction
A quoted lead time is not an installation appointment.
Preserve the decision status
No sample is selected yet.
Summarize the trade-off
The known differences are construction, appearance, and quoted lead time.
6 language and precision notes
More pronounced
Compares the visible grain of these samples without declaring one superior.
More uniform
Describes appearance, not guaranteed consistency of every future piece.
Both
Signals the shared profile rather than identical materials.
Quoted
Attributes the timing to the supplied quotation rather than a universal delivery rule.
Better
Needs a stated criterion before it becomes a meaningful comparison.
Selected
A decision status that must not be inferred from a favorable comment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is fully supported?

Answer and explanation

B. A is solid oak with pronounced grain and ten days; B is veneered with a more uniform look and four days.

The comparison uses the stated construction, appearance, and quoted timing without adding unsupported claims.

2. Which attribute does not distinguish the samples?

Answer and explanation

A. The requested profile, which both match

Both samples meet the requested profile, so it is their shared attribute.

3. Which statement overgeneralizes?

Answer and explanation

C. Veneered trim always arrives in four days.

The four-day quote belongs to sample B, not every veneered product.

4. How should Mei record Oliver's interest in A?

Answer and explanation

A. A is under discussion; no selection has been made.

Interest or discussion alone does not establish selection, purchase, or installation.

E Conversations

Two oak samples, different trade-offs

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Oliver: Client / Mei: Carpenter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • quoted lead time
  • installation date
  • oak veneer
  • performance claim
  • pronounced grain
  • visual preference
  • profile
  • material construction
  • selection status
  • sample approval
  1. Oliver
    Both shapes look right for the study. Before I choose, what is actually different between these samples apart from the price, which I haven't seen?
  2. Mei
    There is a difference in 1. Sample A is solid oak, while sample B is oak-veneered trim. Both match the profile you requested for the study.
  3. Oliver
    A's grain stands out much more against this wall color. B looks quieter. Is that just what we're seeing in these particular pieces?
  4. Mei
    A has 2, and B has a more uniform appearance. Those are the visual differences stated for these samples, not a ranking of quality.
  5. Oliver
    So B has real oak on the surface, over something else? What is underneath it, or don't we have that specification here?
  6. Mei
    Yes, 3 refers to that surface layer. I do not have a specification for the supporting material here, so I should not name a core that is not listed.
  7. Oliver
    Remind me which one has the shorter lead time. I don't want to mistake a supplier quote for the day you'll fit it.
  8. Mei
    The 4 is ten days for A and four days for B. Those are supply quotes, not confirmed installation appointments.
  9. Oliver
    That makes B six days quicker on the quote. I still prefer A's grain, so I'm not ready to choose on timing alone.
  10. Mei
    Exactly. Your 5 and the timing are separate considerations. We can compare them without deciding that one sample is better in every respect.
  11. Oliver
    And I wouldn't lose this edge shape by choosing B? The study trim needs the profile we've already discussed.
  12. Mei
    Both match the requested 6. The known differences here are construction, grain appearance, and quoted lead time, not the shape you specified.
  13. Oliver
    Do we have prices or durability information yet? I don't want to assume veneered automatically means cheaper or less hard-wearing.
  14. Mei
    We do not have a price comparison. I also cannot support a 7 about which lasts longer from the information on these two samples.
  15. Oliver
    I'm leaning toward A, but please don't order it on that basis. I still need the missing details before I approve anything.
  16. Mei
    I will keep the 8 open. We can note your interest in A without recording it as an approved sample or a placed order.
  17. Oliver
    Can you keep the finish details and prices as outstanding questions? They matter to the decision as well as the grain and lead time.
  18. Mei
    We can keep those as separate questions before 9. Nothing in this discussion establishes a finish specification, a price, or a guarantee about future product performance.
  19. Oliver
    Let me read that back: A, solid oak, stronger grain, ten days; B, veneered, more uniform, four days. Neither has an installation booking.
  20. Mei
    Correct, and neither quote is an 10. Both match the profile, no sample is selected, and the other details remain to be confirmed before a decision.

Answers and explanations
  1. material construction Turn 2. Material construction distinguishes solid oak in A from oak veneer over a supporting material in B.
  2. pronounced grain Turn 4. Pronounced grain describes the stronger visible pattern on sample A without making a quality or durability claim.
  3. oak veneer Turn 6. Oak veneer is the thin oak surface layer over a supporting material; it is not the same construction as solid oak.
  4. quoted lead time Turn 8. Quoted lead time is ten days for A and four for B; these figures do not establish installation appointments.
  5. visual preference Turn 10. Visual preference concerns the appearance Oliver favors and remains one consideration alongside the stated timing and construction.
  6. profile Turn 12. Profile is the cross-sectional shape, which both samples match; it is not a difference between these options.
  7. performance claim Turn 14. Performance claim would assert durability or function not supplied by the sample comparison; material names alone do not establish it.
  8. selection status Turn 16. Selection status remains undecided; Oliver's favorable comment about A is not approval or an order instruction.
  9. sample approval Turn 18. Sample approval has not been given; the missing comparisons and Oliver's final choice remain unresolved.
  10. installation date Turn 20. Installation date is not supplied by either lead-time quote, and no sample selection or fitting appointment is confirmed.
Read the complete conversation

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Choosing the study trim reference

At a sample review, a carpenter and client compare the two study trim options.

  1. Carpenter: Both samples match your trim profile. Which difference catches your eye in this light?
  2. Client: Sample A has stronger grain. Is sample B a different shape around the edge?
  3. Carpenter: No, the shape matches. B has an oak veneer and a more uniform appearance.
  4. Client: I prefer A's grain, but the room is needed soon. What about delivery?
  5. Carpenter: The supplier quotes ten days for A and four for B at present.
  6. Client: Does choosing B mean the entire study will be finished six days earlier?
  7. Carpenter: Not necessarily. Those figures concern supply; the site schedule also includes other work.
  8. Client: Then keep A as my preferred sample while you check the scheduling effect.
  9. Carpenter: I'll label it preferred, with the timing decision open, rather than as an order.
  10. Client: Yes, show me that effect before we make the final selection for the room.
White is not a complete finish description

A carpenter and decorator compare a trim sample with an existing painted surface.

  1. Carpenter: The client asked for white trim, but this sample looks glossier than the existing skirting.
  2. Decorator: The color reference matches. The sheen is different, which changes how light reflects.
  3. Carpenter: Could we show both together before calling this a successful finish match today?
  4. Decorator: Certainly. Is the client asking for the same color or the same overall appearance?
  5. Carpenter: Their note says match existing, so I think we need to clarify both.
  6. Decorator: I'll bring a lower-sheen sample with the same color reference for the comparison.
  7. Carpenter: Please identify the sample labels; photographs alone have made the difference hard to judge.
  8. Decorator: Agreed. We can compare them in the room under the same lighting conditions.
  9. Carpenter: Then I'll ask the client to choose a physical reference rather than another vague description.
  10. Decorator: That gives us a specific appearance target without promising every surface will look identical.
The molding is wide enough but different

A carpenter questions a supplier's proposed replacement molding.

  1. Carpenter: The replacement molding is the right width, but the curve doesn't match our sample.
  2. Supplier: Our stock description lists the same overall dimensions. Which feature differs from yours?
  3. Carpenter: The original has a small bead at the edge; the replacement has a flat edge.
  4. Supplier: I see it now in your reference photo. So width alone isn't the issue.
  5. Carpenter: Correct. This piece sits beside retained trim, so the profile difference will be visible.
  6. Supplier: Would you like another stock sample or a quotation for a matching profile?
  7. Carpenter: Please quote the matching profile first, with its lead time shown separately from price.
  8. Supplier: I can do that. The stock alternative can remain available for the client's comparison.
  9. Carpenter: Label it alternative, please, so nobody mistakes it for an accepted substitution on delivery.
  10. Supplier: I'll update the enquiry to show profile match required, with the alternative clearly identified.

Lesson 02: Selecting timber, trim, and visible finishes
Original fictional case: For a study remodel, carpenter Mei compares two trim samples with client Oliver. Sample A is solid oak with pronounced grain and a quoted ten-day lead time. Sample B is oak-veneered trim with a more uniform appearance and a quoted four-day lead time. Both match the requested profile. No sample is selected, and no price comparison, durability ranking, or installation date is supplied. Mei must keep the comparison tied to these samples rather than generalize about every solid or veneered product.
Language workshop: Two oak samples, different trade-offs
Communication goal: Compare specified material, appearance, profile, and quoted lead time without inventing quality rankings or selecting for the client.
Language guidance: Sample A has pronounced grain and a ten-day quoted lead time. Sample B has a more uniform appearance and a four-day quote. Both match the profile. These are sample-specific facts, not universal claims about solid oak and veneered products.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both samples match the requested profile. / Sample A is solid oak. / Sample B is oak-veneered trim. / A has more pronounced grain. / B has a more uniform appearance. / A has a quoted ten-day lead time. / B has a quoted four-day lead time. / B has the shorter quoted lead time for these two samples. / We do not have a price comparison here. / The material names alone do not establish which will perform better. / The profile is not the difference between them. / Is the more visible grain the appearance you prefer? / Discussing A does not mean you have selected it. / A quoted lead time is not an installation appointment. / No sample is selected yet. / The known differences are construction, appearance, and quoted lead time.
Vocabulary: solid oak: Material made from oak wood rather than an oak surface veneer over a core. / oak veneer: Thin oak layer applied over a supporting material. / veneered trim: Trim with a veneer surface over a core. / timber: Wood prepared or used for building and joinery. / trim: Finishing material used at edges, joints, or decorative features. / profile: Cross-sectional shape of a trim piece or molding. / grain: Visible pattern associated with the wood's structure. / pronounced grain: Strongly visible wood pattern. / uniform appearance: More consistent visual character across the sample. / core material: Supporting material beneath a surface layer. / face veneer: Veneer on the visible face of a product. / edge treatment: Way the edge of a component is finished or covered. / finish sample: Example showing a proposed surface appearance. / sheen: Degree of surface reflectivity. / clear finish: Coating that allows underlying material to remain visible. / opaque finish: Coating that hides the underlying material's appearance. / stain: Coloring treatment used to change wood's appearance. / quoted lead time: Supply interval stated in a quotation or proposal. / selection status: Whether a choice has been made. / material construction: How a product is made up of its constituent materials. / visual preference: Appearance favored by the client. / sample approval: Acceptance of a particular reference sample. / performance claim: Statement about how a product will function or endure. / installation date: Scheduled day for fitting work at the site.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with a partner, stressing A, B, ten days, and four days. Switch roles for turns 11-20; separate matching profile from price, performance, and approval. Complete and check the short exchange. Read the corrected comparison without turning a supply quote into an installation date.
Partner: Oliver: Client / Mei: Carpenter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the sample comparison. Use the stated construction, appearance, lead time, and decision status; do not add prices or durability claims.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Oliver: Can you keep the finish details and prices as outstanding questions? They matter to the decision as well as the grain and lead time.
Mei: We can keep those as separate questions before sample approval. Nothing in this discussion establishes a finish specification, a price, or a guarantee about future product performance.
Oliver: Let me read that back: A, solid oak, stronger grain, ten days; B, veneered, more uniform, four days. Neither has an installation booking.
Mei: Correct, and neither quote is an installation date. Both match the profile, no sample is selected, and the other details remain to be confirmed before a decision.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
