03Reading details and resolving drawing conflictsTwo reveals on revision B+

Your goal

Identify a drawing conflict precisely, preserve revision and location details, and request clarification without choosing a dimension by convenience.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Two reveals on revision B

Carpenter Arun and coordinator Lena review cabinet drawings before a fabrication-release review due at 14:00. Elevation C4 revision B shows a 20-millimeter reveal at an end panel. Section S2 revision B shows 15 millimeters at the same panel. The designer has not clarified which dimension applies. Both sheets carry the same revision letter, so the team cannot resolve the discrepancy by assuming one is a later issue. No dimension is authorized through this conversation.

Arun: Carpenter / Lena: Project coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What conflict is shown?

Answer and explanation

A. C4 revision B gives 20 millimeters; S2 revision B gives 15 at the same panel

The two revision-B sheets give different reveal values at the same end panel.

2. What is due at 14:00?

Answer and explanation

A. The fabrication-release review

The supplied time belongs to the review, not a promised answer or completed fabrication.

3. Who has not yet clarified the dimension?

Answer and explanation

A. The designer

The designer has not resolved which dimension applies.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The difference is five millimeters, but calculating that difference does not identify the correct reveal. Both C4 and S2 are revision B and refer to the same panel. Neither sheet is established as superseded or authoritative over the other.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
elevation
Drawing view showing a face of an object or building.read the cabinet elevation
section
Drawing view representing a cut through an object or assembly.compare the section
reveal
Defined visible gap or exposed setback between adjacent components.confirm the reveal dimension
end panel
Panel forming or covering an exposed end of cabinetry.identify the end panel
revision
Identified issue or update of a drawing or document.verify the revision
drawing conflict
Inconsistent information between relevant drawings.flag a drawing conflict
dimension discrepancy
Difference between dimensions that should describe the same detail.report a dimension discrepancy
fabrication
Manufacture of components or assemblies.clarify before fabrication
release review
Review of readiness or permission to issue work for the next stage.attend the release review
shop drawing
Detailed production or installation drawing for a specific assembly.review the shop drawing
detail reference
Identifier pointing to a particular drawing detail.include the detail reference
sheet number
Identifier of a drawing sheet.quote the sheet number
revision cloud
Drawing mark identifying an area of change.locate the revision cloud
superseded drawing
Earlier drawing replaced by a later authorized issue.identify a superseded drawing
design intent
Intended design result communicated through the project information.clarify the design intent
request for information
Formal or recorded query seeking missing or unclear project information.submit a request for information
clarification response
Reply resolving a stated information question.await the clarification response
applicable dimension
Measurement confirmed as relevant to the detail.verify the applicable dimension
fabrication release
Authorization or controlled issue allowing production to proceed.confirm fabrication release
review deadline
Time by which a review is due.state the review deadline
unresolved detail
Information point not yet clarified.flag an unresolved detail
cross-reference
Link between related drawing views or documents.check the cross-reference
kerf
Width of material removed by a cut, distinct from blade plate thickness.allow for the kerf
cut list
List of required parts, quantities, and finished dimensions.review the cut list

14:00 is the fabrication-release review time. It is not an instruction to choose a dimension, a confirmed designer-response time, or evidence that fabrication is released. Refer both values and the precise location through the actual project clarification process.

C Notice the language

Make a conflict easy to answer

A useful clarification request contains both drawing references, their revisions, the exact location, and the conflicting values. Include the review deadline as a coordination fact, not a reason to guess. Shared revision labels do not make incompatible dimensions agree.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Flag the issue
I found a conflict at the cabinet end panel.
Cite the first view
Elevation C4 revision B shows a 20-millimeter reveal.
Cite the second view
Section S2 revision B shows 15 millimeters.
Confirm the shared location
Both dimensions refer to the same end panel.
Check the issue labels
Both sheets are marked revision B.
State the difference
The values differ by five millimeters.
Reject a revision shortcut
We cannot identify a newer sheet from those labels alone.
Name the missing answer
The designer has not clarified which dimension applies.
Request a decision
Please confirm the applicable reveal dimension.
Preserve the review time
The fabrication-release review is due at 14:00.
Avoid a response promise
That review time is not a guaranteed designer-response time.
Keep production status separate
The conflict discussion does not authorize fabrication.
Keep both references
I will include C4 and S2 in the query.
Avoid averaging
I will not use the midpoint as an invented solution.
Ask for an explicit response
The reply needs to identify the dimension for this panel.
Close with the current status
The detail remains unresolved pending clarification.
6 language and precision notes
At the same panel
Establishes that the two values conflict rather than describe separate locations.
Revision B
A shared issue label, not proof that the content agrees.
Applies
Asks which dimension governs the specific detail.
Due for review
Describes a planned decision point without implying approval.
Pending clarification
Keeps the question open until a response resolves it.
Five-millimeter difference
Correct arithmetic that does not answer which value is intended.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which query is complete?

Answer and explanation

A. At the same end panel, C4 rev B shows 20 mm and S2 rev B shows 15 mm; please clarify for the 14:00 release review.

The complete query includes location, both references, conflicting values, and the review time.

2. Which proposed shortcut is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. Average the values and fabricate a 17.5-millimeter reveal.

No authority is given to invent a midpoint or release fabrication from conflicting dimensions.

3. What do the revision labels establish?

Answer and explanation

C. Both sheets are marked B, not which dimension is correct

The shared letter does not identify precedence or resolve the inconsistent detail.

4. Which status is accurate at the end?

Answer and explanation

D. The reveal remains unresolved and fabrication is not authorized by this conversation.

No designer clarification or fabrication authorization is supplied during the exchange.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Two reveals on revision B

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Arun: Carpenter / Lena: Project coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • fabrication release
  • design intent
  • review deadline
  • clarification response
  • applicable dimension
  • drawing conflict
  • dimension discrepancy
  • sheet number
  • superseded drawing
  • unresolved detail
  1. Arun
    Lena, can we stop on the end-panel detail before the release review? The elevation and section give different reveals at the same panel.
  2. Lena
    Give me each 1 and revision, please. I want the designer to find the conflict without having to guess which cabinet we're discussing.
  3. Arun
    Elevation C4, revision B: twenty millimeters. Section S2, also revision B: fifteen. I've checked that they point to this same end panel.
  4. Lena
    That's a five-millimeter 2. Has the designer answered it in a separate message, or are we still waiting for the intended reveal?
  5. Arun
    Still waiting. The workshop has asked which figure to use. I can't choose twenty just because the elevation is the clearer view.
  6. Lena
    Nor can we choose fifteen for convenience. We need a 3 identifying the intended value and the detail it applies to.
  7. Arun
    Someone thought S2 might be the old sheet. Both labels say B, though, and I haven't found anything establishing that either has been replaced.
  8. Lena
    Then don't mark it a 4. Matching revision letters don't resolve a conflict or tell us that the elevation takes precedence.
  9. Arun
    The fabrication-release review is at fourteen hundred. Can the query say that, so the designer understands the timing without reading the whole schedule?
  10. Lena
    I'll include the 5. It's the review time, not a promised response time, and it doesn't give us permission to pick a dimension.
  11. Arun
    Put both values in the actual question, please. If the message just says end-panel query, it may come back asking for the missing numbers.
  12. Lena
    I'll state the 6 in one sentence: C4 rev B shows twenty, S2 rev B shows fifteen, at the same end panel.
  13. Arun
    And ask specifically for the reveal. I don't want this turned into a request to recheck every dimension in the cabinet package.
  14. Lena
    Agreed. The 7 is the reveal at this location. A precise query doesn't imply we've verified the rest of the package.
  15. Arun
    If the answer hasn't arrived by the review, what goes in the record? Leaving the box empty could make it look resolved.
  16. Lena
    Record an 8, with the query reference. No reply is not approval of either figure, and the conflicting values must remain visible.
  17. Arun
    The other suggestion was seventeen and a half, splitting the difference. That would keep everyone moving, but it's not on either drawing.
  18. Lena
    Averaging isn't 9. Seventeen and a half would be a third, invented dimension, not a resolution of the two we were given.
  19. Arun
    Understood. I'll bring both sheets to the fourteen-hundred review and keep the reveal outstanding. Can you send the specific question now?
  20. Lena
    Yes. The query doesn't grant 10. I'll preserve both references, values, and the unresolved status so the release decision isn't based on an assumption.

Answers and explanations
  1. sheet number Turn 2. Sheet number locates each drawing precisely for the designer's review of the conflict.
  2. dimension discrepancy Turn 4. Dimension discrepancy is 20 versus 15 millimeters at the same end panel, not two unrelated measurements.
  3. clarification response Turn 6. Clarification response must resolve the intended dimension; convenience or drawing readability does not establish the correct value.
  4. superseded drawing Turn 8. Superseded drawing would mean an earlier issue had been replaced; the shared B labels do not establish that status.
  5. review deadline Turn 10. Review deadline is 14:00 for the fabrication-release review, not an agreed designer-response time or automatic production permission.
  6. drawing conflict Turn 12. Drawing conflict keeps both incompatible values and their references visible in the handoff.
  7. applicable dimension Turn 14. Applicable dimension concerns the reveal at this end panel, not every cabinet measurement.
  8. unresolved detail Turn 16. Unresolved detail keeps the unanswered reveal question visible instead of allowing silence to imply that it has been settled.
  9. design intent Turn 18. Design intent requires clarification; averaging the conflicting values invents a result instead of establishing it.
  10. fabrication release Turn 20. Fabrication release is not granted here; the reveal still requires designer clarification.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Arun: Lena, can we stop on the end-panel detail before the release review? The elevation and section give different reveals at the same panel.
  2. Lena: Give me each sheet number and revision, please. I want the designer to find the conflict without having to guess which cabinet we're discussing.
  3. Arun: Elevation C4, revision B: twenty millimeters. Section S2, also revision B: fifteen. I've checked that they point to this same end panel.
  4. Lena: That's a five-millimeter dimension discrepancy. Has the designer answered it in a separate message, or are we still waiting for the intended reveal?
  5. Arun: Still waiting. The workshop has asked which figure to use. I can't choose twenty just because the elevation is the clearer view.
  6. Lena: Nor can we choose fifteen for convenience. We need a clarification response identifying the intended value and the detail it applies to.
  7. Arun: Someone thought S2 might be the old sheet. Both labels say B, though, and I haven't found anything establishing that either has been replaced.
  8. Lena: Then don't mark it a superseded drawing. Matching revision letters don't resolve a conflict or tell us that the elevation takes precedence.
  9. Arun: The fabrication-release review is at fourteen hundred. Can the query say that, so the designer understands the timing without reading the whole schedule?
  10. Lena: I'll include the review deadline. It's the review time, not a promised response time, and it doesn't give us permission to pick a dimension.
  11. Arun: Put both values in the actual question, please. If the message just says end-panel query, it may come back asking for the missing numbers.
  12. Lena: I'll state the drawing conflict in one sentence: C4 rev B shows twenty, S2 rev B shows fifteen, at the same end panel.
  13. Arun: And ask specifically for the reveal. I don't want this turned into a request to recheck every dimension in the cabinet package.
  14. Lena: Agreed. The applicable dimension is the reveal at this location. A precise query doesn't imply we've verified the rest of the package.
  15. Arun: If the answer hasn't arrived by the review, what goes in the record? Leaving the box empty could make it look resolved.
  16. Lena: Record an unresolved detail, with the query reference. No reply is not approval of either figure, and the conflicting values must remain visible.
  17. Arun: The other suggestion was seventeen and a half, splitting the difference. That would keep everyone moving, but it's not on either drawing.
  18. Lena: Averaging isn't design intent. Seventeen and a half would be a third, invented dimension, not a resolution of the two we were given.
  19. Arun: Understood. I'll bring both sheets to the fourteen-hundred review and keep the reveal outstanding. Can you send the specific question now?
  20. Lena: Yes. The query doesn't grant fabrication release. I'll preserve both references, values, and the unresolved status so the release decision isn't based on an assumption.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two dimensions on revision B

A carpenter calls the designer before the cabinet fabrication review.

  1. Carpenter: Could you clarify the end-panel reveal before our two o'clock fabrication review today?
  2. Designer: Which drawings disagree? I need their references and revisions to locate the issue.
  3. Carpenter: Elevation C4 shows twenty millimeters; section detail S2 shows fifteen. Both are revision B.
  4. Designer: Are those dimensions at the same end panel, or on opposite cabinet runs?
  5. Carpenter: The same panel beside the study doorway. I've marked that location on both.
  6. Designer: Thank you. I need to reconcile the detail, and I cannot answer before three.
  7. Carpenter: Then I'll keep that panel out of release at the two o'clock review.
  8. Designer: Please distinguish it from the other panels, which aren't part of this query.
  9. Carpenter: I will. Can your reply identify both the intended reveal and the drawing correction?
  10. Designer: Yes. My response will address those points, with the marked references attached for clarity.
Extra width is not an extra shelf

A carpenter explains a supplier's scribe allowance note to a client.

  1. Carpenter: The supplier's end panel is wider on the schedule than on your finished elevation.
  2. Client: That's what worried me. Will it make the cupboard stick farther into the room?
  3. Carpenter: The schedule includes a scribe allowance, meaning extra material for fitting the adjoining surface.
  4. Client: So the scheduled piece size is not necessarily the finished visible panel width?
  5. Carpenter: Exactly. We still need the detail to confirm how that allowance is represented.
  6. Client: Please ask them, because I don't want the finished cupboard width quietly increased.
  7. Carpenter: I'll ask for separate supplied and finished dimensions on this panel's confirmation drawing.
  8. Client: Can we keep the agreed cabinet face line as the reference in that question?
  9. Carpenter: Yes, I'll name that line explicitly and attach the elevation you already approved.
  10. Client: Then the question is about interpreting the allowance, not approving a larger cupboard today.
A cloud on the drawing

A carpenter and site coordinator identify the actual extent of a revised drawing.

  1. Carpenter: The latest elevation has a revision cloud around the upper shelves. Did the base change?
  2. Site coordinator: The issue note mentions shelf spacing only, but let's check the revision description together.
  3. Carpenter: Our earlier base detail still appears in the folder beside this new elevation.
  4. Site coordinator: That's possible; not every sheet was reissued. Which base dimension are you concerned about?
  5. Carpenter: The drawer front height. I don't see a changed dimension or a corresponding note.
  6. Site coordinator: Then we shouldn't assume the cloud changes that height merely because it's nearby.
  7. Carpenter: Could you confirm the current sheet list so the workshop uses the right combination?
  8. Site coordinator: Yes. I'll identify the reissued elevation and the retained base detail on the transmittal.
  9. Carpenter: I'll tell the workshop that shelf spacing changed, while base-height clarification remains separate.
  10. Site coordinator: Good. That prevents a partial drawing issue from being interpreted as an entirely new cabinet.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two reveals on revision B
  1. Read turns 1-10; pronounce both sheet references, revision letters, and millimeter values distinctly.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress unresolved and keep the 14:00 review distinct from release.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Refer the conflicting detail

Complete the drawing query using both references and values. Keep the shared revision and the unresolved status intact.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Carpenter: "Elevation ___ revision B shows 20 millimeters."

Answer and explanation

C. C4

C4 is the elevation reference carrying the 20-millimeter reveal.

2. Coordinator: "Section S2 revision B shows ___ millimeters."

Answer and explanation

D. 15

Fifteen millimeters is the conflicting value on section S2.

3. Carpenter: "Both refer to the same end ___."

Answer and explanation

C. panel

The shared panel location is why the values conflict.

4. Coordinator: "The designer has not yet ___ the applicable dimension."

Answer and explanation

A. clarified

No clarification has resolved the conflict or authorized a dimension.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Reading details and resolving drawing conflicts
Original fictional case: Carpenter Arun and coordinator Lena review cabinet drawings before a fabrication-release review due at 14:00. Elevation C4 revision B shows a 20-millimeter reveal at an end panel. Section S2 revision B shows 15 millimeters at the same panel. The designer has not clarified which dimension applies. Both sheets carry the same revision letter, so the team cannot resolve the discrepancy by assuming one is a later issue. No dimension is authorized through this conversation.
Language workshop: Two reveals on revision B
Communication goal: Identify a drawing conflict precisely, preserve revision and location details, and request clarification without choosing a dimension by convenience.
Language guidance: The difference is five millimeters, but calculating that difference does not identify the correct reveal. Both C4 and S2 are revision B and refer to the same panel. Neither sheet is established as superseded or authoritative over the other.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I found a conflict at the cabinet end panel. / Elevation C4 revision B shows a 20-millimeter reveal. / Section S2 revision B shows 15 millimeters. / Both dimensions refer to the same end panel. / Both sheets are marked revision B. / The values differ by five millimeters. / We cannot identify a newer sheet from those labels alone. / The designer has not clarified which dimension applies. / Please confirm the applicable reveal dimension. / The fabrication-release review is due at 14:00. / That review time is not a guaranteed designer-response time. / The conflict discussion does not authorize fabrication. / I will include C4 and S2 in the query. / I will not use the midpoint as an invented solution. / The reply needs to identify the dimension for this panel. / The detail remains unresolved pending clarification.
Vocabulary: elevation: Drawing view showing a face of an object or building. / section: Drawing view representing a cut through an object or assembly. / reveal: Defined visible gap or exposed setback between adjacent components. / end panel: Panel forming or covering an exposed end of cabinetry. / revision: Identified issue or update of a drawing or document. / drawing conflict: Inconsistent information between relevant drawings. / dimension discrepancy: Difference between dimensions that should describe the same detail. / fabrication: Manufacture of components or assemblies. / release review: Review of readiness or permission to issue work for the next stage. / shop drawing: Detailed production or installation drawing for a specific assembly. / detail reference: Identifier pointing to a particular drawing detail. / sheet number: Identifier of a drawing sheet. / revision cloud: Drawing mark identifying an area of change. / superseded drawing: Earlier drawing replaced by a later authorized issue. / design intent: Intended design result communicated through the project information. / request for information: Formal or recorded query seeking missing or unclear project information. / clarification response: Reply resolving a stated information question. / applicable dimension: Measurement confirmed as relevant to the detail. / fabrication release: Authorization or controlled issue allowing production to proceed. / review deadline: Time by which a review is due. / unresolved detail: Information point not yet clarified. / cross-reference: Link between related drawing views or documents. / kerf: Width of material removed by a cut, distinct from blade plate thickness. / cut list: List of required parts, quantities, and finished dimensions.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Discussing concealed conditions without guessingStaining is not a structural finding+

Your goal

Describe an exposed condition, explain what cannot be seen, and refer a structural concern without diagnosing concealed timber.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Staining is not a structural finding

At a laundry remodel, client Theo notices dark staining on exposed subfloor beside the laundry doorway. He asks carpenter Nia whether the joists are rotten. The joists are not visible, and Nia has not assessed structural condition. Project lead Sam can arrange a qualified assessment. Nia must describe the visible staining and the limits of her knowledge without confirming decay, declaring the structure sound, identifying a cause, or promising a repair.

Theo: Client / Nia: Carpenter

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is visible?

Answer and explanation

B. Dark staining on exposed subfloor beside the laundry doorway

Only the staining on the exposed subfloor is directly visible.

2. What limits Nia's conclusion?

Answer and explanation

D. The joists are not visible and structural condition has not been assessed

The concealed joists and lack of structural assessment prevent a supported diagnosis.

3. What can Sam arrange?

Answer and explanation

B. A qualified assessment

Sam can arrange assessment, but no finding, repair, or price is supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The visible staining is on the exposed subfloor, not on visible joists. Theo raises possible rot as a question. The joists are concealed and structural condition has not been assessed, so neither decay nor structural soundness is established.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
subfloor
Supporting floor layer beneath the finished floor covering.describe the exposed subfloor
joist
Structural member supporting a floor or ceiling over a span.refer to the concealed joists
floor covering
Visible finish material laid over the floor base.distinguish the floor covering
exposed surface
Area visible after a covering is absent or removed.describe the exposed surface
concealed condition
State of material or construction hidden from view.refer a concealed-condition concern
dark staining
Visible discoloration with a dark appearance.report dark staining
discoloration
Change in the apparent color of a surface.describe the discoloration
timber decay
Deterioration of wood through biological action.avoid diagnosing timber decay
structural condition
State of components relevant to carrying loads.request assessment of structural condition
load-bearing member
Component intended to support structural loads.identify the load-bearing member
moisture source
Origin of water or dampness affecting an area.investigate the moisture source
water ingress
Entry of water into an area or construction.report a concern about water ingress
inspection access
Ability to reach or view an area for examination.confirm inspection access
visibility limit
Boundary of what can actually be seen.explain the visibility limit
qualified assessor
Person competent to examine the relevant condition.refer to a qualified assessor
assessment scope
Matters included in a specified examination.confirm the assessment scope
site observation
Condition directly noticed at the location.record a site observation
unsupported diagnosis
Conclusion about a problem without adequate assessment.avoid an unsupported diagnosis
structural finding
Conclusion reached through appropriate structural assessment.distinguish a structural finding
repair proposal
Suggested work to address an established issue.await a repair proposal
investigation status
Current stage of checking an unresolved concern.report the investigation status
laundry doorway
Opening at the entrance to the laundry area.locate the laundry doorway
condition report
Record describing an examined or observed state.prepare an accurate condition report
referral route
Process for passing a concern to the appropriate person.explain the referral route

The location and appearance can be recorded without naming a cause. Sam can arrange a qualified assessment, but the dialogue does not authorize opening up construction, select a testing method, or establish a repair scope, price, or timetable.

C Notice the language

Take the concern seriously without guessing

A worried client may hear uncertainty as evasiveness. Give a concrete observation and explain the visibility limit before naming the assessment route. Neither alarming certainty nor unsupported reassurance helps the client understand what is actually known.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the observation
I can see dark staining on the exposed subfloor.
Locate it precisely
It is beside the laundry doorway.
Acknowledge the concern
I understand why you are asking about the joists.
State the visibility limit
The joists are not visible here.
State the assessment limit
I have not assessed the structural condition.
Avoid a diagnosis
I cannot confirm that the joists are rotten.
Avoid false reassurance
I cannot declare them sound from this view either.
Keep cause open
The cause of the staining has not been established.
Separate surfaces
The visible subfloor and the concealed joists are different parts.
Refer the concern
Sam can arrange a qualified assessment.
Avoid a repair promise
No repair scope or price is established.
Preserve the client's question
I will include your concern about possible decay.
Use neutral wording
Dark staining observed; concealed condition unassessed.
Avoid an invented finding
There is no structural finding to report yet.
Keep the next step clear
The next step is assessment, not a guessed repair.
Read back the record
Staining beside the laundry doorway, with joists not visible and cause unknown.
6 language and precision notes
Rotten
A diagnosis the client asks about, not a finding the worker can confirm.
Exposed versus concealed
Distinguishes what can be seen from what remains hidden.
Cannot confirm
Limits certainty without asserting that the concern is impossible.
Either
Helps balance an unsupported negative conclusion with an unsupported reassurance.
Arrange
Names a next step without claiming it has already happened.
Unassessed
Means no relevant assessment has been made, not that the condition is acceptable.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report separates observation from diagnosis?

Answer and explanation

D. Dark staining is visible on the subfloor; the concealed joists have not been assessed.

The report states the visible fact and preserves the unassessed concealed condition.

2. Which answer to Theo is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The joists are definitely sound because we cannot see damage.

Inability to see the joists cannot establish that they are structurally sound.

3. Which location should the referral include?

Answer and explanation

D. Beside the laundry doorway

The supplied location helps the assessor identify the actual observed area.

4. What remains unknown?

Answer and explanation

C. The cause, concealed structural condition, and any required repair

No assessment has established the cause, structural condition, or appropriate repair.

E Conversations

Staining is not a structural finding

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Theo: Client / Nia: Carpenter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • moisture source
  • repair proposal
  • investigation status
  • visibility limit
  • referral route
  • dark staining
  • qualified assessor
  • laundry doorway
  • site observation
  • subfloor
  1. Theo
    Nia, this dark patch by the laundry door looks worrying. Is that rot in the joists, or am I jumping to conclusions?
  2. Nia
    What I can identify is 1 on the exposed subfloor. The joists aren't visible, and I haven't assessed the structural condition.
  3. Theo
    I thought what we're looking at might be the joists themselves. Are those different from this exposed layer?
  4. Nia
    Yes. This is the 2; the joists beneath it are concealed. A mark here doesn't tell us whether those hidden members have decay.
  5. Theo
    So you can record the stain, but not my guess about what caused it as though it's a finding?
  6. Nia
    Exactly. I'll separate the 3 from your question: visible discoloration here, and a concern about the condition of the concealed joists.
  7. Theo
    Could it be coming from the laundry? That was my first thought because it's so close to the doorway.
  8. Nia
    The 4 isn't established. The location is useful to report, but it doesn't prove a leak or tell us where any water came from.
  9. Theo
    Who should look into it? I'd like someone qualified to assess it before I'm told the floor needs replacing.
  10. Nia
    I'll contact Sam to arrange a 5. The referral will give the location, what is exposed, and what we cannot currently see.
  11. Theo
    Does that mean replacement is likely? I'm trying to work out whether we need to budget for a much bigger job.
  12. Nia
    We don't have a 6 yet. Asking for an assessment doesn't establish replacement, a price, or the disruption involved.
  13. Theo
    Would it be reasonable to say the joists are probably fine until someone finds damage? Or is that going too far as well?
  14. Nia
    The 7 works both ways. I can't confirm decay, but hidden joists also prevent me from assuring you that the structure is sound.
  15. Theo
    All right. Please don't describe today's conversation as a structural inspection. I can imagine that getting repeated later as an all-clear.
  16. Nia
    I'll keep the 8 explicit: structural condition not assessed. The visible concern is being referred, not diagnosed or signed off.
  17. Theo
    Can you read the location back? There are two laundry doors, and I don't want the person coming out to review the wrong patch.
  18. Nia
    The note identifies the exposed subfloor beside this 9, with the staining shown in the referral record. It also states that the joists are concealed.
  19. Theo
    Yes, that's the area. Please give Sam the question as well as the observation, so it doesn't become just a cosmetic stain report.
  20. Nia
    I will use that 10 and include your concern about possible decay. I won't add a repair conclusion or a claim of structural safety.

Answers and explanations
  1. dark staining Turn 2. Dark staining is the observed discoloration on the exposed subfloor, not a confirmed finding of decay in concealed joists.
  2. subfloor Turn 4. Subfloor is the visible supporting floor layer in this case, distinct from the concealed joists about which Theo asks.
  3. site observation Turn 6. Site observation preserves the visible staining and its location without turning the client's proposed explanation into a diagnosis.
  4. moisture source Turn 8. Moisture source has not been established; the question about water does not prove where it came from or what it affected.
  5. qualified assessor Turn 10. Qualified assessor is the appropriate person to examine the relevant concern; no completed assessment or finding exists yet.
  6. repair proposal Turn 12. Repair proposal is not established by requesting an assessment; replacement, cost, and disruption have not been determined.
  7. visibility limit Turn 14. Visibility limit prevents both a confirmed decay diagnosis and a claim that concealed structural members are sound.
  8. investigation status Turn 16. Investigation status remains unassessed for structural condition; describing staining is not equivalent to completing an inspection.
  9. laundry doorway Turn 18. Laundry doorway is the specific location of the observation, allowing the referral to identify the relevant area accurately.
  10. referral route Turn 20. Referral route passes the concern to Sam for arranging qualified assessment, without inventing a diagnosis or authorizing a repair.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Theo: Nia, this dark patch by the laundry door looks worrying. Is that rot in the joists, or am I jumping to conclusions?
  2. Nia: What I can identify is dark staining on the exposed subfloor. The joists aren't visible, and I haven't assessed the structural condition.
  3. Theo: I thought what we're looking at might be the joists themselves. Are those different from this exposed layer?
  4. Nia: Yes. This is the subfloor; the joists beneath it are concealed. A mark here doesn't tell us whether those hidden members have decay.
  5. Theo: So you can record the stain, but not my guess about what caused it as though it's a finding?
  6. Nia: Exactly. I'll separate the site observation from your question: visible discoloration here, and a concern about the condition of the concealed joists.
  7. Theo: Could it be coming from the laundry? That was my first thought because it's so close to the doorway.
  8. Nia: The moisture source isn't established. The location is useful to report, but it doesn't prove a leak or tell us where any water came from.
  9. Theo: Who should look into it? I'd like someone qualified to assess it before I'm told the floor needs replacing.
  10. Nia: I'll contact Sam to arrange a qualified assessor. The referral will give the location, what is exposed, and what we cannot currently see.
  11. Theo: Does that mean replacement is likely? I'm trying to work out whether we need to budget for a much bigger job.
  12. Nia: We don't have a repair proposal yet. Asking for an assessment doesn't establish replacement, a price, or the disruption involved.
  13. Theo: Would it be reasonable to say the joists are probably fine until someone finds damage? Or is that going too far as well?
  14. Nia: The visibility limit works both ways. I can't confirm decay, but hidden joists also prevent me from assuring you that the structure is sound.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A stain is not a joist assessment

At the laundry doorway, a carpenter answers the client's question about staining.

  1. Carpenter: There's dark staining on the exposed subfloor beside the laundry doorway in this area.
  2. Client: Does that mean the joists underneath are rotten and the whole floor needs replacing?
  3. Carpenter: I can't conclude that. The joists aren't visible, and their condition hasn't been assessed.
  4. Client: We had a washing-machine spill years ago. Could that explain what you're seeing now?
  5. Carpenter: It could be relevant history, but I can't confirm the cause from the stain.
  6. Client: Please include the spill when you speak to the project lead about the assessment.
  7. Carpenter: I will describe it as your recollection, separately from the visible staining we see.
  8. Client: Will that assessment also tell us whether any change to the agreed work is needed?
  9. Carpenter: The assessment can clarify this concealed condition before we discuss a revised carpentry scope.
  10. Client: All right. I'd rather understand the finding than approve replacement based on appearance alone.
The photograph does not show the support

A carpenter and project lead discuss an incomplete site photograph.

  1. Carpenter: The client's photograph shows the new shelf location, but not what's behind the wall.
  2. Project lead: They're asking whether the existing blocking can support the cabinet at that position.
  3. Carpenter: We don't have a verified record of blocking there, so the photograph cannot answer.
  4. Project lead: Is there an earlier framing record we can request from the original contractor first?
  5. Carpenter: Yes, I'll ask for that record and identify which wall and cabinet position we mean.
  6. Project lead: Please don't describe the missing record as evidence that no blocking exists at all.
  7. Carpenter: Agreed. I'll say the support arrangement remains unverified, rather than absent or inadequate.
  8. Project lead: The client also wants a fixed installation date. What can we tell them today?
  9. Carpenter: We can give the current planning status and explain which assessment the date depends on.
  10. Project lead: I'll handle that conversation and keep the support question separate from our scheduling preference.
A small gap with no agreed tolerance

A carpenter discusses a reported trim gap with the client's representative.

  1. Carpenter: You noted a gap at the stair trim. Which joint are you referring to?
  2. Client representative: The upper corner beside the landing. I photographed it this morning from the hallway.
  3. Carpenter: Thank you. Do we have an agreed sample or acceptance detail for that joint?
  4. Client representative: There's an approved detail, but I haven't compared the photograph with it yet.
  5. Carpenter: Then let's identify the location and request a comparison before calling it acceptable or defective.
  6. Client representative: The owner asked me whether every gap of that size is always a defect.
  7. Carpenter: I wouldn't give a universal answer. The detail and actual condition matter for this review.
  8. Client representative: I'll send the photograph with the drawing reference, without presenting my impression as a finding.
  9. Carpenter: Please also note the viewing location so the reviewer understands what the image shows.
  10. Client representative: Done. We'll describe it as a reported finish concern pending comparison with the agreed detail.

Answer and explanation

The joists remain concealed, so their condition cannot be judged from this view.

No structural assessment has occurred in the supplied case facts.

Assessment is the available next step, not a confirmed diagnosis or repair.

Lesson 04: Discussing concealed conditions without guessing
Original fictional case: At a laundry remodel, client Theo notices dark staining on exposed subfloor beside the laundry doorway. He asks carpenter Nia whether the joists are rotten. The joists are not visible, and Nia has not assessed structural condition. Project lead Sam can arrange a qualified assessment. Nia must describe the visible staining and the limits of her knowledge without confirming decay, declaring the structure sound, identifying a cause, or promising a repair.
Language workshop: Staining is not a structural finding
Communication goal: Describe an exposed condition, explain what cannot be seen, and refer a structural concern without diagnosing concealed timber.
Language guidance: The visible staining is on the exposed subfloor, not on visible joists. Theo raises possible rot as a question. The joists are concealed and structural condition has not been assessed, so neither decay nor structural soundness is established.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can see dark staining on the exposed subfloor. / It is beside the laundry doorway. / I understand why you are asking about the joists. / The joists are not visible here. / I have not assessed the structural condition. / I cannot confirm that the joists are rotten. / I cannot declare them sound from this view either. / The cause of the staining has not been established. / The visible subfloor and the concealed joists are different parts. / Sam can arrange a qualified assessment. / No repair scope or price is established. / I will include your concern about possible decay. / Dark staining observed; concealed condition unassessed. / There is no structural finding to report yet. / The next step is assessment, not a guessed repair. / Staining beside the laundry doorway, with joists not visible and cause unknown.
Vocabulary: subfloor: Supporting floor layer beneath the finished floor covering. / joist: Structural member supporting a floor or ceiling over a span. / floor covering: Visible finish material laid over the floor base. / exposed surface: Area visible after a covering is absent or removed. / concealed condition: State of material or construction hidden from view. / dark staining: Visible discoloration with a dark appearance. / discoloration: Change in the apparent color of a surface. / timber decay: Deterioration of wood through biological action. / structural condition: State of components relevant to carrying loads. / load-bearing member: Component intended to support structural loads. / moisture source: Origin of water or dampness affecting an area. / water ingress: Entry of water into an area or construction. / inspection access: Ability to reach or view an area for examination. / visibility limit: Boundary of what can actually be seen. / qualified assessor: Person competent to examine the relevant condition. / assessment scope: Matters included in a specified examination. / site observation: Condition directly noticed at the location. / unsupported diagnosis: Conclusion about a problem without adequate assessment. / structural finding: Conclusion reached through appropriate structural assessment. / repair proposal: Suggested work to address an established issue. / investigation status: Current stage of checking an unresolved concern. / laundry doorway: Opening at the entrance to the laundry area. / condition report: Record describing an examined or observed state. / referral route: Process for passing a concern to the appropriate person.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with a partner; distinguish the visible subfloor from the concealed joists. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Retain both limits: no confirmed decay and no assurance of structural soundness. Check the transfer answers, then read the referral with the location and Sam's role intact.
Partner: Theo: Client / Nia: Carpenter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the report to the project lead. Keep the observed surface separate from the concealed joists and the unperformed assessment.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Theo: Can you read the location back? There are two laundry doors, and I don't want the person coming out to review the wrong patch.
Nia: The note identifies the exposed subfloor beside this laundry doorway, with the staining shown in the referral record. It also states that the joists are concealed.
Theo: Yes, that's the area. Please give Sam the question as well as the observation, so it doesn't become just a cosmetic stain report.
Nia: I will use that referral route and include your concern about possible decay. I won't add a repair conclusion or a claim of structural safety.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Carpentry & Remodeling English
Professional audience: Carpenters, remodelers, finish carpenters, joinery installers, and carpentry crew leads
Scope: All clients, drawings, materials, dimensions, products, prices, schedules, and site conditions in these cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not structural assessment, building-code compliance, tool operation, demolition, installation, or legal interpretation. Follow actual project documents, manufacturer instructions, site controls, competent assessments, and applicable requirements. The scenarios do not authorize fabrication from conflicting drawings, entry to unreleased areas, concealed-condition work, substitutions, extra work, or changes to warranty terms.
Lesson 04: Discussing concealed conditions without guessing
Original fictional case: At a laundry remodel, client Theo notices dark staining on exposed subfloor beside the laundry doorway. He asks carpenter Nia whether the joists are rotten. The joists are not visible, and Nia has not assessed structural condition. Project lead Sam can arrange a qualified assessment. Nia must describe the visible staining and the limits of her knowledge without confirming decay, declaring the structure sound, identifying a cause, or promising a repair.
Language workshop: Staining is not a structural finding
Communication goal: Describe an exposed condition, explain what cannot be seen, and refer a structural concern without diagnosing concealed timber.
Language guidance: The visible staining is on the exposed subfloor, not on visible joists. Theo raises possible rot as a question. The joists are concealed and structural condition has not been assessed, so neither decay nor structural soundness is established.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can see dark staining on the exposed subfloor. / It is beside the laundry doorway. / I understand why you are asking about the joists. / The joists are not visible here. / I have not assessed the structural condition. / I cannot confirm that the joists are rotten. / I cannot declare them sound from this view either. / The cause of the staining has not been established. / The visible subfloor and the concealed joists are different parts. / Sam can arrange a qualified assessment. / No repair scope or price is established. / I will include your concern about possible decay. / Dark staining observed; concealed condition unassessed. / There is no structural finding to report yet. / The next step is assessment, not a guessed repair. / Staining beside the laundry doorway, with joists not visible and cause unknown.
Vocabulary: subfloor: Supporting floor layer beneath the finished floor covering. / joist: Structural member supporting a floor or ceiling over a span. / floor covering: Visible finish material laid over the floor base. / exposed surface: Area visible after a covering is absent or removed. / concealed condition: State of material or construction hidden from view. / dark staining: Visible discoloration with a dark appearance. / discoloration: Change in the apparent color of a surface. / timber decay: Deterioration of wood through biological action. / structural condition: State of components relevant to carrying loads. / load-bearing member: Component intended to support structural loads. / moisture source: Origin of water or dampness affecting an area. / water ingress: Entry of water into an area or construction. / inspection access: Ability to reach or view an area for examination. / visibility limit: Boundary of what can actually be seen. / qualified assessor: Person competent to examine the relevant condition. / assessment scope: Matters included in a specified examination. / site observation: Condition directly noticed at the location. / unsupported diagnosis: Conclusion about a problem without adequate assessment. / structural finding: Conclusion reached through appropriate structural assessment. / repair proposal: Suggested work to address an established issue. / investigation status: Current stage of checking an unresolved concern. / laundry doorway: Opening at the entrance to the laundry area. / condition report: Record describing an examined or observed state. / referral route: Process for passing a concern to the appropriate person.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with a partner; distinguish the visible subfloor from the concealed joists. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Retain both limits: no confirmed decay and no assurance of structural soundness. Check the transfer answers, then read the referral with the location and Sam's role intact.
Partner: Theo: Client / Nia: Carpenter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the report to the project lead. Keep the observed surface separate from the concealed joists and the unperformed assessment.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Theo: Can you read the location back? There are two laundry doors, and I don't want the person coming out to review the wrong patch.
Nia: The note identifies the exposed subfloor beside this laundry doorway, with the staining shown in the referral record. It also states that the joists are concealed.
Theo: Yes, that's the area. Please give Sam the question as well as the observation, so it doesn't become just a cosmetic stain report.
Nia: I will use that referral route and include your concern about possible decay. I won't add a repair conclusion or a claim of structural safety.
END REFERENCE
05Separating requested extras from agreed workA bench beyond the skirting scope+

Your goal

Explain the agreed task, clarify an additional request, and discuss a separate quotation without implying approval or completion timing.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A bench beyond the skirting scope

At a bedroom remodel, carpenter Jules reviews the scope with client Amara. The agreed work is replacing skirting only. Decorating is excluded. Amara asks for a built-in bench as an extra. Jules can arrange a separate quotation, but no bench dimensions, price, or completion date are agreed. The conversation must distinguish the existing trim work from the requested bench and must not imply that decorating or a free extra is included.

Amara: Client / Jules: Carpenter

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is included in the current scope?

Answer and explanation

D. Replacing bedroom skirting only

The agreed scope is limited to replacing the bedroom skirting.

2. What is the new request?

Answer and explanation

A. A built-in bench

Amara requests a built-in bench in addition to the existing work.

3. What can Jules arrange?

Answer and explanation

A. A separate quotation, with dimensions, price, and date still unagreed

The separate quotation route is available, but the bench terms have not been established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Replacing the bedroom skirting is the only agreed task. The bench is an additional request, and decorating is excluded. A separate quotation can be arranged, but the request itself does not establish bench measurements, an accepted price, or a completion date.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
skirting
Trim along the base of an internal wall, often called baseboard.replace the skirting
baseboard
Common North American term for trim at the foot of an internal wall.compare baseboard profiles
built-in bench
Fixed seating integrated into a room or fitted assembly.request a built-in bench
agreed scope
Work explicitly included in the current agreement.confirm the agreed scope
scope exclusion
Work or condition left outside the agreement.explain the scope exclusion
decorating
Surface finishing work such as painting or wallpapering.distinguish decorating from carpentry
additional work
Task requested beyond the existing scope.quote for additional work
separate quotation
Distinct price proposal for specified extra work.arrange a separate quotation
bench dimensions
Measurements defining the proposed bench.agree the bench dimensions
design brief
Statement of the requirements for a proposed design.clarify the design brief
seat height
Vertical position of a seat surface from a stated reference.confirm the seat height
seat depth
Front-to-back dimension of a seat surface.clarify the seat depth
overall length
Full end-to-end measurement of an item.confirm the overall length
finish requirement
Specified surface appearance or treatment.clarify the finish requirement
quotation scope
Tasks and terms covered by a price proposal.define the quotation scope
variation
Agreed or proposed change to the original work arrangement.record a proposed variation
change authorization
Permission to alter the agreed work.obtain change authorization
price agreement
Acceptance of a stated cost for defined work.confirm the price agreement
completion commitment
Agreed promise about when work will finish.avoid an unsupported completion commitment
book matching
Pairing consecutive veneer leaves by alternating their faces for mirrored grain.specify book matching
measurement visit
Attendance to establish relevant dimensions.discuss a measurement visit
client approval
Acceptance from the client through the relevant process.record client approval
existing contract
Current agreement governing the booked work.distinguish the existing contract
scope handoff
Transfer of the task description for further review or quotation.make a clear scope handoff

Do not convert a sketch, preference, or informal conversation into an agreed design. The case provides no bench dimensions or finish specification. Keep the current task intact while passing the extra request through the actual quotation and approval process.

C Notice the language

A helpful referral can preserve a firm scope limit

An additional request often arises when a client sees the room taking shape. Acknowledge the idea, identify the existing agreement, and offer the actual quotation route. Avoid making the client feel dismissed while also avoiding a casual promise that creates a different expectation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the idea
I understand you would like a built-in bench as well.
Name the agreement
The current scope is replacing the bedroom skirting only.
State the exclusion
Decorating is excluded from this booking.
Separate the extra
The bench is additional work, not part of the existing task.
Offer the next step
I can arrange a separate quotation.
Keep dimensions open
We have not agreed the bench dimensions.
Keep price open
There is no agreed bench price yet.
Keep timing open
No completion date is confirmed for the bench.
Avoid a free-extra promise
I cannot treat the bench as a free addition.
Avoid bundling decorating
A bench request does not add painting to the current scope.
Clarify the proposal
We need a defined bench request for the quotation.
Separate asking and approving
Asking for a quote does not approve the extra work.
Preserve existing work
The skirting replacement remains the agreed task.
Avoid designing by assumption
I will not put guessed measurements into the handoff.
Read back the request
Built-in bench requested; dimensions, price, and timing unresolved.
Close with the route
I will pass the bench request for a separate quotation.
6 language and precision notes
Only
Makes the limit of the current skirting scope explicit.
As well
Introduces an addition without proving it is included.
Quote for
Describes pricing a defined proposal rather than authorizing the work.
Not yet agreed
Preserves the possibility of a later agreement without inventing one now.
Excluded
Applies to decorating in the current booking, not necessarily every future service.
Proposed variation
Identifies a possible change before it becomes an authorized one.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which scope summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Skirting replacement only; decorating excluded; bench requested separately.

The summary preserves the existing task, the exclusion, and the separate new request.

2. Which response is helpful without overpromising?

Answer and explanation

A. I can arrange a separate quotation, with dimensions and timing still to be agreed.

The quotation route acknowledges the request while preserving the unresolved bench details.

3. What does requesting a quote establish?

Answer and explanation

D. Interest in having the extra work priced, not approval to perform it

A quotation request is not acceptance of terms or authorization of the additional work.

4. Which item must remain excluded from the current booking?

Answer and explanation

A. Decorating

Decorating is explicitly excluded and is not added by the bench discussion.

A bench beyond the skirting scope

Amara: Client / Jules: Carpenter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  1. Amara
    While you're doing the bedroom skirting, could you also build a bench along that wall? I'm wondering whether it could be added to this visit.
  2. Jules
    I can pass on the idea, but the 1 is skirting replacement only. A built-in bench would be a separate item rather than part of the existing task.
  3. Amara
    I realize it isn't another piece of skirting. I thought being here already might make it simpler, but I haven't worked out the details.
  4. Jules
    It is 2, and we need a defined proposal for it. Being on site does not establish the dimensions, the price, or when it could be completed.
  5. Amara
    Could I get a separate price before I decide? I'm asking for an option, not telling you to start building it.
  6. Jules
    I can arrange a 3. Asking for that quote does not mean you have approved the bench or agreed to pay an amount we have not supplied.
  7. Amara
    I haven't settled the length or depth. When I pointed at the wall, I didn't mean that the bench must fill the whole space.
  8. Jules
    I will keep the 4 unresolved. The handoff will describe a built-in bench request without turning your gesture into a fixed length or depth.
  9. Amara
    What about painting? I know decorating isn't in the present booking. Would a bench quote need to say explicitly whether finishing is included?
  10. Jules
    The current 5 remains decorating. The bench request does not add painting to today's agreement, and any proposed finish would need to be stated in its own quotation.
  11. Amara
    Yes, I'd rather see what's covered than compare prices that mean different things. The finish could affect which proposal I accept.
  12. Jules
    A clear 6 matters. We should not promise a finish, a design, or a service that has not been defined in the proposal.
  13. Amara
    Could it be finished this week? I'm asking whether that's possible, but I don't want you to put a date into the quote without checking.
  14. Jules
    No 7 has been made for the bench. I cannot give you a confirmed date before the proposed work and arrangements are agreed.
  15. Amara
    Then today's skirting job stays as agreed while the bench is priced separately. Is there anything in this discussion that changes the existing booking?
  16. Jules
    I will preserve the 8 scope. The skirting remains the agreed task, and the bench will be recorded as a separate request for pricing.
  17. Amara
    Good. Please don't take my interest as approval. I'll review the defined proposal and price before giving a decision on the extra work.
  18. Jules
    Correct. 9 has not happened in this conversation. I will not describe your interest as approval to start building or to choose measurements for you.
  19. Amara
    Please pass on: built-in bedroom bench requested for a separate quote; size, finish, price, and timing unresolved. No instruction to proceed.
  20. Jules
    I will make that 10 clearly. The note will keep the bench proposal separate, preserve the decorating exclusion, and leave the unagreed details open for the quotation process.

Answers and explanations
  1. agreed scope Turn 2. Agreed scope is bedroom skirting replacement only; the bench request does not automatically belong to that agreement.
  2. additional work Turn 4. Additional work describes the bench beyond the existing skirting task, even though both would be in the same bedroom.
  3. separate quotation Turn 6. Separate quotation is the available route for pricing the bench request; it is not an accepted price or permission to begin.
  4. bench dimensions Turn 8. Bench dimensions are not agreed; a pointing gesture does not establish length, depth, height, or a final design.
  5. scope exclusion Turn 10. Scope exclusion keeps decorating outside the existing booking; the bench discussion does not silently add painting or other finishing work.
  6. quotation scope Turn 12. Quotation scope identifies what a proposed price covers, including any stated finish requirements, rather than relying on an unstated assumption.
  7. completion commitment Turn 14. Completion commitment for the bench is absent; interest in the idea and a quotation referral do not establish a finish date.
  8. existing contract Turn 16. Existing contract continues to cover the skirting replacement; discussing a bench does not cancel or replace that task.
  9. Change authorization Turn 18. Change authorization has not been given; a future decision would need to establish the additional work through the relevant process.
  10. scope handoff Turn 20. Scope handoff transfers the bench request with its unresolved dimensions, price, and timing while preserving the skirting-only agreement.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The bench is an addition

A carpenter and bedroom client discuss the requested bench.

  1. Carpenter: The bedroom quotation covers skirting, or baseboard, replacement. The bench would be additional work.
  2. Client: I thought there might be room in the price because it's quite small.
  3. Carpenter: We haven't agreed its dimensions or storage arrangement, so I can't price it yet.
  4. Client: Could you get a separate quotation without changing the skirting work already agreed?
  5. Carpenter: Yes. We also need to clarify making good, because decorating is currently excluded.
  6. Client: I'd like a painted finish, with space underneath for two storage boxes.
  7. Carpenter: Please give us the box dimensions so that requirement is clear in the enquiry.
  8. Client: Does asking for the quotation mean I have committed to buying the bench?
  9. Carpenter: No. A change order would record any agreed alteration after you consider the price and timing.
  10. Client: Then obtain that comparison, and keep the existing bedroom scope unchanged for now.
Painting around the new frame

A carpenter and homeowner clarify restoration around a replacement frame.

  1. Carpenter: The frame proposal mentions making good locally. What finish were you expecting?
  2. Homeowner: I assumed the whole wall would be repainted so no difference would show.
  3. Carpenter: The quotation doesn't define that extent clearly. Let's clarify it before accepting the scope.
  4. Homeowner: Can you explain the difference between local making good and decorating the wall?
  5. Carpenter: Local work concerns disturbed areas; whole-wall decoration is a broader item to price.
  6. Homeowner: Then I'd like both extents described, because appearance matters more than a vague allowance.
  7. Carpenter: I'll ask the estimator to identify the surfaces and finish standard for each option.
  8. Homeowner: Please include the return beside the doorway; it's easy to overlook from this room.
  9. Carpenter: I'll name it separately so we can see whether each quotation includes it.
  10. Homeowner: Good. We'll decide from those descriptions instead of assuming the word locally covers everything.
A verbal suggestion becomes a request

A carpenter checks a shelving conversation with a tenant representative.

  1. Carpenter: Yesterday you mentioned another shelf above the desk. Was that a suggestion or request?
  2. Tenant representative: A request for a price, not approval to add it to the order.
  3. Carpenter: Thanks. The workshop heard add a shelf and asked me for fabrication dimensions.
  4. Tenant representative: Please tell them to hold that addition. The facilities manager approves extra costs here.
  5. Carpenter: I'll keep the approved shelving separate and request a price for the additional piece.
  6. Tenant representative: Can the quotation also say whether the extra shelf changes the delivery timing?
  7. Carpenter: Yes, that question belongs in the comparison rather than being assumed unchanged.
  8. Tenant representative: The facilities manager is available Thursday. I can discuss the priced option then.
  9. Carpenter: I'll label it quotation requested, with your review meeting noted as the next decision point.
  10. Tenant representative: That reflects what I intended and avoids the workshop treating a conversation as approval.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Course: Carpentry & Remodeling English
Professional audience: Carpenters, remodelers, finish carpenters, joinery installers, and carpentry crew leads
Scope: All clients, drawings, materials, dimensions, products, prices, schedules, and site conditions in these cases are fictional.
