Dialogue 05: One extra room, two possible packages
Original fictional setting: A remodeling carpenter and owner discuss extending a trim package to a second room.
Professional roles: Carpenter / Owner
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Carpenter: You asked about adding the dining room. The current package covers the sitting room only.
Owner: Yes. I'd like matching trim in both, but I need a useful comparison before deciding.
Carpenter: We can quote the dining room separately or request a combined revision covering both rooms.
Owner: Would the combined revision automatically include repainting the doors as well as the new trim?
Carpenter: No. We should list door painting explicitly if you want it included in that comparison.
Owner: Please include it as a separate line. I might keep the existing door finish after all.
Carpenter: I'll distinguish trim supply, fitting, and the requested decorating extent, with timing for each proposed package.
Owner: The estimate has an allowance. Doesn't that mean the extra room is already partly paid for?
Carpenter: That allowance is identified for the sitting-room trim selection, not an unspecified amount of extra work.
Owner: Understood. Keep that reference visible so I can compare the original scope with the proposed addition.
Carpenter: I will ask for the difference to be shown clearly, without treating either proposal as accepted.
Owner: Then I'll review both packages with the decorator before approving any extension of the work.
Vocabulary in this exchange: allowance: An amount included in a project budget for a specified item whose final selection or cost is not yet settled.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Course: Carpentry & Remodeling English

Dialogue 06: A handover between the floor and cabinet teams
Original fictional setting: A carpenter and flooring supervisor settle the boundaries of an upcoming room release.
Professional roles: Carpenter / Flooring supervisor
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Carpenter: The lookahead says kitchen available Friday. Does that mean your work and room release are both complete?
Flooring supervisor: Our floor installation is planned to finish Friday, but the room-release conditions still need confirmation.
Carpenter: Thanks. The phrase available sounded broader than that when the cabinet supplier read the schedule yesterday.
Flooring supervisor: I understand. We should replace it with a clear status, including what remains to be checked.
Carpenter: Can you give the coordinator that distinction today? The delivery booking needs a decision tomorrow morning.
Flooring supervisor: Yes. I'll report installation timing separately from access release and name the person confirming the latter.
Carpenter: The supplier also asks whether their delivery route crosses the retained hallway finish beside the kitchen.
Flooring supervisor: It does. That interface belongs in the protection discussion, rather than being left to the delivery crew.
Carpenter: I'll include it in the coordination call, with the agreed hallway restrictions visible to everyone attending.
Flooring supervisor: Good. Please avoid telling the client the cabinets are delayed by flooring before the options are reviewed.
Carpenter: Agreed. Let's confirm access and check the delivery options before saying the flooring team has delayed us.
Flooring supervisor: Then our update will identify the open release condition and tomorrow's booking decision without assigning unsupported blame.
END REFERENCE

Course: Carpentry & Remodeling English

Dialogue 07: A finish concern after the review meeting
Original fictional setting: A carpenter and client agree how to handle a new snag reported after an earlier walkthrough.
Professional roles: Carpenter / Client
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Carpenter: You sent a photograph after yesterday's walkthrough. Which feature would you like reviewed?
Client: The joint above the left corner looks uneven in daylight, although I didn't notice it last evening.
Carpenter: Thank you. I'll identify that joint and the lighting difference rather than say the entire trim failed.
Client: Will raising it now cancel the other items we agreed were satisfactory during the walkthrough?
Carpenter: Not automatically in our review record. We can keep those observations and add this specific concern separately.
Client: I'd like that. The rest of the room looks good, and I don't want the issue exaggerated.
Carpenter: Do you have the original image available? The messaging app preview makes the corner hard to see.
Client: Yes, I'll send the full image and indicate the left corner on a second copy for orientation.
Carpenter: I'll ask the joinery lead to review it against the detail, with the cause and remedy still open.
Client: Please include me in the appointment. I want to show the exact view before anything is proposed.
Carpenter: Thursday at ten is available for that review. Is that suitable for you to attend in person?
Client: Yes. Confirm Thursday's review, and describe it as the window-joint concern rather than a complete room rejection.
END REFERENCE

Client: Yes, I'll send the full image and indicate the left corner on a second copy for orientation.
Carpenter: I'll ask the joinery lead to review it against the detail, with the cause and remedy still open.
Client: Please include me in the appointment. I want to show the exact view before anything is proposed.
Carpenter: Thursday at ten is available for that review. Is that suitable for you to attend in person?
Client: Yes. Confirm Thursday's review, and describe it as the window-joint concern rather than a complete room rejection.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 08: The owner needs a useful final record
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Carpentry & Remodeling English
Professional audience: Carpenters, remodelers, finish carpenters, joinery installers, and carpentry crew leads
Scope: All clients, drawings, materials, dimensions, products, prices, schedules, and site conditions in these cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not structural assessment, building-code compliance, tool operation, demolition, installation, or legal interpretation. Follow actual project documents, manufacturer instructions, site controls, competent assessments, and applicable requirements. The scenarios do not authorize fabrication from conflicting drawings, entry to unreleased areas, concealed-condition work, substitutions, extra work, or changes to warranty terms.
Dialogue 08: The owner needs a useful final record
Original fictional setting: A carpenter and facilities representative review a remodeled meeting room's handover pack.
Professional roles: Carpenter / Facilities representative
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Carpenter: The pack contains cabinet records, finish references, and supplier documents. One trim item remains open.
Facilities representative: Which item? I need to explain the room status to our incoming colleague.
Carpenter: The short molding beside the store door is awaiting replacement. Everything else on this list has been reviewed.
Facilities representative: Please keep review status separate from warranty terms; our incoming colleague sometimes confuses them.
Carpenter: I will. The warranty document is separate, and any coverage question should refer to its actual terms.
Facilities representative: Is the molding's return visit booked, or are we still waiting for the supplier to confirm availability?
Carpenter: Availability is still pending. Joel owns the follow-up and has agreed to update you on Monday afternoon.
Facilities representative: Can the incoming colleague be included, so the handover doesn't depend on forwarding my messages later?
Carpenter: Yes. With your agreed contact details, I'll include them and ask them to acknowledge receipt of the pack.
Facilities representative: Their name is Lena. I'll introduce her today and explain that the molding remains an open item.
Carpenter: Then Monday's update will go to both of you, without presenting the room record as a completion guarantee.
Facilities representative: Lena will have the documents, the open item, and a named contact to follow up with.
Vocabulary in this exchange: warranty document: A document stating the provider's warranty terms and conditions for identified work or products. / return visit: A planned later attendance to review or complete identified outstanding work.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

REFERENCE
Course: Carpentry & Remodeling English
Professional audience: Carpenters, remodelers, finish carpenters, joinery installers, and carpentry crew leads
Scope: All clients, drawings, materials, dimensions, products, prices, schedules, and site conditions in these cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not structural assessment, building-code compliance, tool operation, demolition, installation, or legal interpretation. Follow actual project documents, manufacturer instructions, site controls, competent assessments, and applicable requirements. The scenarios do not authorize fabrication from conflicting drawings, entry to unreleased areas, concealed-condition work, substitutions, extra work, or changes to warranty terms.
Lesson 01: Confirming openings and measurement references
Original fictional case: Client Hana and carpenter Ellis review a hall drawing labeled door opening: 900 millimeters. Hana assumes the figure describes clear passage width. The note does not identify a rough or finished opening, and no door has been ordered. Ellis must clarify the ambiguity without inventing a frame allowance or asserting a usable width. Designer Priya can explain which reference the dimension uses. The conversation concerns interpretation and referral, not a construction method or a compliance decision.
Language workshop: What does 900 millimeters describe?
Communication goal: Identify an unclear measurement reference, correct an assumption, and obtain designer clarification before treating the dimension as usable passage.
Language guidance: The only established figure is 900 millimeters in an unclear opening note. Rough opening, finished opening, door size, and clear passage are not interchangeable labels. No conversion or allowance is supplied, and no door has been ordered.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The hall drawing labels the door opening as 900 millimeters. / Are you reading that as the clear passage width? / The note does not say rough or finished opening. / The opening reference and the usable passage are not necessarily the same. / What surfaces does this dimension run between? / I am not changing the figure of 900 millimeters. / We do not have a confirmed frame allowance here. / No door has been ordered. / Priya can clarify the measurement reference. / I cannot confirm 900 millimeters of clear passage from this note. / We should not treat this as the order size yet. / Could the clarification identify the opening type? / I will explain that your question concerns usable passage. / This conversation does not establish compliance. / Does 900 refer to the rough opening, finished opening, or another reference? / I will request clarification before presenting the dimension as a confirmed usable width.
Vocabulary: opening dimension: Measurement describing an opening using a stated reference. / rough opening: Structural opening before the door assembly and relevant finishes are installed. / finished opening: Opening measured at the specified completed surfaces. / clear passage: Unobstructed usable space through an opening under the stated conditions. / door leaf: Moving panel of a door. / door frame: Surrounding assembly supporting and locating the door. / jamb: Frame member; side jambs and the head jamb identify different positions. / head: Upper horizontal member or part of an opening. / threshold: Lower part at the base of a doorway. / casing: Trim around a door or window opening. / architrave: Term commonly used for decorative trim around an opening. / millimeter: Metric unit equal to one thousandth of a meter. / dimension line: Drawing line indicating the extent of a measurement. / reference face: Surface from which a dimension is measured. / datum: Stated reference point, line, or level for measurements. / nominal size: Designated size that may differ from an actual measured size. / actual dimension: Measured or specifically defined physical size. / frame allowance: Space allowed for the specified frame arrangement. / clearance: Space between components or around an operating part. / door schedule: Drawing or document listing specified doors and their attributes. / drawing note: Written instruction or information on a drawing. / designer clarification: Explanation from the responsible design professional. / order size: Dimensions used to specify a product for purchase. / dimension reference: Point, surface, or condition a measurement describes.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, stressing opening, passage, and ordered when each distinction changes the meaning. Switch roles for turns 11-20; quote 900 exactly and keep Priya's clarification separate from a width guarantee. Complete and check the four-line exchange. Read it aloud without subtracting an invented frame allowance.
Partner: Hana: Client / Ellis: Carpenter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the exchange about the hall drawing. Keep the given number, missing reference, unverified passage, and designer referral distinct.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: And we're not confirming that the doorway meets a particular access requirement today? I don't want to pass that on as settled.
Ellis: Correct. Resolving the drawing note isn't a compliance assessment. We'll keep that distinction clear when we pass the question to Priya.
Hana: Then the message is: what does 900 measure, and what usable passage will this arrangement give? No order until the reference is resolved.
Ellis: Yes. The actual dimension of that passage remains unverified. I'll send the query with the original note, without promising a width or a product size.
END REFERENCE

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.
